Backup bench and design cases — use only if a primary doesn't fit UPDATED

Four fallbacks for the bar raiser, none of them another interviewer's primary: S19 and S20 are design cases — not lived results (the Walmart Marketplace delivery-promise material is my own interview-prep case work on illustrative data; every number is "in the model", and the lived record for the theme is S8 / Apple workbook F4) — plus two doc-derived Flexport benches from the program record: the RAPID Crux customer transition (Earn Trust / Customer Obsession: 79 customers, transition-or-cancel decision rules) and the FFA Asia latency investigation (Dive Deep; facts identical to Hoerger's bench) — both record-from-docs, both from the S11/S12 program family, so keep the shared numbers identical to Tripathy's packet. S13 (Cyber Monday) and S9 (597 lanes first) were promoted from this bench to full primaries in v2. Tell S14, S16, S10, S15, S13 and S9 first; a bar raiser compares notes, so a bench story must carry exactly the master's numbers.

Bench discipline. The six primaries are the stories to tell first. Reach for a bench story only when the question genuinely does not fit a primary, or when the interviewer asks for "another example" on an LP already spent. Each entry is a 60-second pitch, a compressed STAR, one results line and two probes — enough to answer and stop; no full probing bank or derivation. Where a bench story is another interviewer's primary, the red note says so: use it only if the question demands it, and keep the facts identical to that packet.

Design case — not a lived resultS19 — Design case: a marketplace delivery-promise buffer as one governed dial — in the model, +32 points of fast-promise share at the same 95% floor (all model; no delivered dollar)

LPs this design case can serve: Dive Deep (alternate when S8/S10 are spent) · Invent and Simplify (alternate) · Frugality (speed bought with zero new spend) — design-flavored probes; always as "I designed / in the model / here is how I would", never as a result I delivered
Basis — a design case, not a lived result. This is a case I worked through in my own interview preparation for a marketplace delivery-promise role, on an illustrative twelve-cohort model (war book and solver model; sample data). I did not deliver these results for any employer, and I never configured or deployed any platform. Every quantity is "in the model" — say it in the same breath; there is no production dollar and none is ever promoted. The lived record for promise-at-the-tail is S8 (Elementum; Apple workbook F4): 200 lanes, 99% lead-time accuracy, 580,000 units and about $1.5M of inventory carrying cost released — state the carry rate when you say it. Say that one if he wants a result. Labels (metro two-day, mountain rural) are the model's illustrative cohort names.
60-second pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation (the case). A marketplace printing delivery dates on inventory it never touches: promised date = order time + seller handling + predicted transit + a safety buffer — and in the case the buffer is a flat 1.10 days on every lane regardless of variability. In the model — twelve illustrative lane cohorts, about a million orders a week, transit σ from 0.22 to 1.60 days — that flat buffer prints 95.8% accuracy but puts only 7.6% of orders on a fast promise: dense metro lanes over-insured and fat-tailed rural lanes under-insured at the same time. Task (as I designed it). Own the decomposition, the alternatives, the validation gates and the governance recommendation. Action (what I designed). Discovery: the buffer-waste audit — the flat buffer buys Φ(5.0), near-certainty, on the tightest cohort and only Φ(0.69) ≈ 75% on the widest. Plan & Design: four policies on one comparison table — uniform, σ-proportional with one governed dial, minimum-days at the floor, profit-optimal on priced breach economics. Build & Test: b = 1.645σ holds 95% on every cohort by construction; the equimarginal check proves the optimum; the Q4 drill shows a frozen summer dial breaking the floor. Gates I would set: four shadow weeks against logged live orders before any displayed promise; the dial published in the weekly review header and re-solved before peak. Result (in the model). Fast-promise share 7.6% → 39.6%, +32 points at the held 95% floor; average promised day 3.09 → 2.75; +$255K a week, about $13.3M a year — all in the model, on an illustrative book. It is a design, not a delivered result, and I say so in the same breath.

Compressed STAR
  • S — A flat 1.10-day buffer on every lane: 95.8% headline accuracy hiding over-insurance on tight lanes and under-insurance on loose ones; fast-promise share 7.6% (in the model).
  • T — Design case: decompose the waste, put priced alternatives on one table, design the gates, leave a governance instrument — not a tuning habit.
  • A — Promise identity and accuracy factorization; buffer-waste audit; four policies on one table; proofs (Φ(1.645) = 0.95 per cohort, equimarginal check, Q4 drill at ~1.45× σ); shadow mode for four weeks (designed); the dial k owned by a business owner and printed every Monday.
  • R — In the model: fast-promise share 7.6 → 39.6% (+32 pts) at exactly the 95% floor; average promised day 3.09 → 2.75; +$255K/wk ≈ $13.3M/yr — modeled, labeled; the widest cohorts get honestly slower promises (1.10 → 2.63 days) on purpose.
Results line

In the model: fast-promise share 7.6% → 39.6% (+32.0 points) at a held 95.0% floor · average promised day 3.09 → 2.75 (model) · +$255K/wk ≈ $13.3M/yr vs uniform (modeled on the illustrative ~1M-order book) · the profit-optimal frontier (97.8% accuracy, ≈ +$20M/yr) handed to Finance as a priced decision — shown, never claimed, never merged with the $13.3M.

3Incumbent bufferb = 1.10 days on every laneuniform policy A (illustrative model)1.10 d everywhere
4Tightest cohort, z-scorez = b / σ = 1.10 / 0.22 = 5.0; Φ(5.0) ≈ 0.9999997metro cohort σ = 0.22 d (illustrative model)near-certainty bought — promise needlessly slow
5Widest cohort, z-scorez = 1.10 / 1.60 = 0.6875; Φ(0.69) ≈ 0.754mountain-rural cohort σ = 1.60 d (illustrative model)≈75.4% kept — a known-loss book

7The asymmetrypadding fails silently (lost conversion files no ticket); cutting fails loudly (breaches do)design, from my prep docs — why the system drifted slowbias toward slow, unowned

2Tightest cohort re-bufferedb = 1.645 × 0.22 = 0.36 daysσ = 0.22 d (illustrative)buffer 1.10 → 0.36 d; 0.74 d of speed harvested

4Every cohort at the floorP(kept) = Φ(1.645 × σ / σ) = Φ(1.645) = 0.95 on each cohortby construction (design)95.0% everywhere; no cohort subsidizes another

Two probes

The whole story rests on an illustrative cohort set.

It does, deliberately — the model exists so the argument happens in numbers instead of adjectives. If the real σ looks nothing like the model's, k changes and the lane ranking changes; what survives any parameter set is the structure: buffers risk-proportional, the last buffer-day buying the same accuracy everywhere, and a floor that disagrees with the breach economics treated as a governance finding.

Why one dial instead of per-lane learned buffers?

In the design I would put per-lane learned buffers on the same table as a priced row — more model, more value, invisible governance — and recommend the one-dial policy first: it captures most of the value, it is auditable, and σ improvements flow into speed automatically and visibly. The difference sits in the comparison table as a priced row, not a meeting minute.

Design-case numbers (all in the model; basis labels): 7.6% → 39.6% fast-promise share (+32 pts, model) · 95.8% → 95.0% accuracy held at the floor (model) · 3.09 → 2.75 promised days (model) · +$255K/wk ≈ $13.3M/yr (modeled) · 12 cohorts, ~1M orders/wk, σ 0.22–1.60 days, k = 1.645 (illustrative model) · breach ≈$12 vs margin ≈$6.50 (model assumptions). Never a production dollar; never a delivered result.

Basis — a design case, not a lived result. I designed the weekly business review on paper — metric architecture, decomposition math, thresholds and control loop — as part of my own interview preparation for a marketplace delivery-promise role, on illustrative sample data (six sample weeks). I did not stand it up for any employer, never configured or deployed any platform, and never claim a production output metric moved. 60-second pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation (the case). A marketplace whose delivery promise is owned by a committee: data science owns the model, engineering the engine, seller ops the handling, carrier programs the lanes — and promise accuracy, the one number the customer feels, is owned by nobody between Tuesdays. Scorecards run on 30-day rolling windows, so a degrading seller or carrier does weeks of customer damage before any floor bites, and a red week becomes a blame negotiation. Task (as I designed it). Design the weekly business review — outputs a customer feels, input levers a named owner can turn, and the math that connects them — plus a control loop faster than the 30-day clock. Action (what I designed). Discovery: one glossary, one event source, audited denominators. Plan & Design: seven output metrics over ten input levers, each input with a roll-up and a named owner; the factorization — accuracy ≈ seller-ships-in-window × carrier-meets-transit × Φ(buffer/σ) — as the decomposition; red/green at ±5% relative week-over-week with hard reds on policy floors. Proof: the sample week — 0.97 × 0.985 × 0.996 ≈ 95.2% — and the drill: a regional carrier miss (97% nationally, 82% in the Southeast) decomposed to its factor in minutes. Cadence: Monday data freeze, Tuesday review, reds pre-wired with cause, owner, date. Control: mix-adjusted twins next to every raw output, and the Andon — a 7-day control chart per seller-lane cell, act within 24 hours. Result (in the model). Red-week attribution from days of negotiation to minutes by factor math, proven on the sample-data drill; detection-to-action from ~30 days to under 24 hours by design. A design case — nothing here is a delivered production result.

Compressed STAR
  • S — Output metric owned by a committee; 30-day rolling windows smearing step changes into drift; three teams keeping three clocks; a red week discovered live and negotiated.
  • T — Design the WBR for the pre-purchase delivery experience — metric architecture, decomposition math, thresholds and control loop — as a design case, with owners named so it can be handed over.
  • A — One glossary and denominator audit; 7 outputs over 10 input levers with roll-ups and named owners; the factor identity as the decomposition; ±5% WoW red/green with hard reds on floors; Monday freeze → Tuesday review, reds pre-wired; mix-adjusted twins; a cell-grain Andon (7-day EWMA/CUSUM, 2× baseline breach, n ≥ 30, act in 24 h, false-trip bar < 5%).
  • R — In the model: attribution days → minutes; detection-to-action ~30 days → < 24 h (designed); Promise Yield 43.6% × 95.2% ≈ 41.5% baseline on the sample set, four-quarter design target 50%; ≈$0.58M per caught episode (modeled).
Results line

Red-week attribution: a cross-team negotiation → minutes by factor math (proven on the sample-data drill) · detection-to-action ~30 days → under 24 hours by design (the Andon) · 7 outputs / 10 inputs / 1 joint headline / 6 sample weeks (my design) · ≈$0.58M per caught degradation episode (modeled; no annual figure — episode frequency is unknown and I won't invent it).

Two probes

Your baseline Promise Yield is sample data.

Correct, and I say so: 43.6% × 95.2% ≈ 41.5% is the case docs' own illustrative six-week set, used to prove the mechanics before any live line exists. The 50% target is a design target for a real book, and the first live job would be re-baselining it.

How is this different from the Flexport review (S11)?

S11 is the production record — a review I ran by hand every Monday for over a year (automation 40 → 90%, accuracy 60 → 90%). This design case is where I carried the review's mathematics — the factor decomposition and the control loop — furthest, as a design. I keep the two separate and never borrow a number across them.

Basis. Doc-derived micro-story from the program record (RAPID — the Crux customer transition, 2022) — every figure is record-from-docs. 60-second pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. The acquired visibility product, Crux, still carried 79 customers — $185,892 ARR and 294,581 containers a year — and the decision was taken to move them onto Shipment Visibility by end 2022. The catch: SV did not fit every segment; some customers were better served by an honest cancel than a force-fit. Task. Decide every customer — transition or cancel — without burning the revenue that fit or the trust of those that didn't. Action. Segment-level decision rules instead of 79 one-offs: truck brokers to a white-label partner product or cancel; forwarders, customs brokers and 3PLs to the SV API or cancel; the two competitor SCM-SaaS accounts canceled on published dates (August 1 and October 24, 2022). The two largest accounts by volume got per-customer calls — one moving a national home-improvement retailer's freight, the other a 600-truck carrier relationship. Internal pushback — migration keep-the-lights-on cost, feature parity, the timing of consignee acquisition — was answered with priced options in the decision document, not overridden by title. Result. Every one of the 79 customers had a decided, dated path — transition where SV fit, cancel honestly where it did not — with the segment table and the decision rules as the record. record-from-docs

Compressed STAR
  • S — 79 acquired-product customers ($185,892 ARR; 294,581 containers) across eight segments, on a product being retired by end 2022; the replacement did not fit every segment.
  • T — Own the transition design: decision rules per segment, dates per account, and the handling of internal dissent.
  • A — A segment table with a transition-or-cancel rule per segment; a white-label partner product for truck brokers; the SV API for forwarders/brokers/3PLs; competitor SaaS accounts canceled on published dates; per-customer calls for the two largest-volume accounts; pushback on KTLO cost, feature parity and consignee timing priced into the decision document.
  • R — 79 of 79 customers decided and dated (record-from-docs). The record carries no preserved-ARR figure, and none is claimed.

Two probes

How do you justify canceling a paying customer?

By pricing the alternative honestly: force-fitting a segment the product doesn't serve converts this year's revenue into next year's churn plus support cost, and it burns the reference. A clean cancel with notice and a path — a white-label partner product, or the API if they fit it — kept the relationship honest; two of the 79 were competitor SaaS accounts where canceling was the only truthful option.

How did you handle the internal pushback?

As inputs to price, not objections to defeat: migration KTLO cost, feature parity and consignee-acquisition timing each got an option with a cost in the decision document, and the decider chose with the prices on the table. Pushback that is priced becomes design input; pushback that is overridden becomes a later surprise.

Enterprise 14 · SMB 39 · Motor Carrier 9 (record-from-docs) · two SaaS cancels dated Aug 1 / Oct 24, 2022 (record-from-docs) · transition deadline end 2022 (record-from-docs). Backup — only if askedDoc bench — Flexport FFA — the Asia latency investigation: a 21-second P95 traced to routing, decomposed to the layer, fixed in sequence (Dive Deep)

LPs this backup can serve: Dive Deep (primary — decomposition to the layer) · Deliver Results · Ownership (the standing rule imported into later launches) · Insist on the Highest Standards (a smoke-test protocol per change)
Basis. Doc-derived micro-story from the program record (the FFA Asia latency investigation, post-MVP 2022) — every figure is record-from-docs: the investigation document, written when the FF App MVP had launched to 11 partners in 10 countries and 48 of 53 import partners (90.5%) had migrated. Program-lead voice: I owned the investigation, the decomposition and the fix sequence; infrastructure teams executed the WAF and CDN changes. Not in the master packet's S-numbering; supplements canon, never overrides it. The same bench appears in Hoerger's packet — facts identical.
60-second pitch — stop on the primary metric

Situation. Weeks after the FF App MVP launched to 11 partners in 10 countries — with 48 of 53 import partners already migrated — the monthly business review showed Asia page loads at a P95 of 21.19 seconds against 3.3 in the US. Task. Find the layer, not the anecdote, and fix it without a re-architecture in the middle of a live partner-facing launch. Action. I decomposed the problem before prescribing: static assets ran ~5s P95 in China against ~500ms in the US, and the app's domain had no geo-based DNS or reverse proxy — Asia traffic was riding the public internet across the Pacific; a double CDN sat on the static path. The fix was a sequence, not a project: enable the WAF, remove the double CDN (direct to S3), then a regional CDN proposal with a smoke-test protocol per change, then China tuning, then Asia tuning — each with a date and an owner. Result. Root cause isolated to routing — not the app, not the partners; two fixes shipped inside the investigation window and the regional plan scheduled with dates; and a standing rule imported into every later launch: the pilot's geography is a performance requirement, not a rollout detail. record-from-docs

Compressed STAR
  • S — Post-MVP, the MBR surfaced Asia P95 page load at 21.19s vs 3.3s in the US (17-day measurement window); 48 of 53 import partners (90.5%) already migrated — the app had to hold where the partners were.
  • T — Isolate the layer causing Asia latency and sequence the fix set without destabilizing a live partner-facing launch.
  • A — Split static from dynamic: static JS ~5s P95 in China vs ~500ms US; found no geo-based DNS / reverse proxy on the domain (public-internet routing) and a double CDN on static assets; sequenced WAF enablement → double-CDN removal (direct to S3) → regional CDN proposal → China tuning → Asia tuning, each gated by a smoke-test protocol with a named date and owner.
  • R — Root cause isolated to routing; WAF and double-CDN fixes shipped inside the window; CDN / China / Asia work scheduled with owners; the investigation became the template for launch-geography checks.
Results line

P95 page load 21.19s Asia vs 3.3s US (record-from-docs) · static ~5s China vs ~500ms US (record-from-docs) · 48 of 53 partners migrated = 90.5% (record-from-docs; canon's "48 of 53 within a month" — consistent) · two fixes shipped, three sequenced with dates (record-from-docs) · no post-fix latency figure exists in the record — never invent one.

Two probes

How did you know it was routing and not the application?

By decomposition before remediation: the static/dynamic split put ~5s of the China time on assets the app server never touched, and the same assets served in ~500ms from the US — the code was constant, the path was not. The domain's missing geo-DNS / reverse proxy and the double CDN explained both halves of the gap without a single application change.

Why not just stand up a China region?

A region is a re-architecture with a compliance tail; the evidence said the bottleneck was routing and CDN topology, which fixes in weeks. The sequenced plan — WAF, double-CDN removal, regional CDN, tuning — bought most of the win at a fraction of the risk, and the smoke-test protocol meant each step was reversible.

Canon numbers (basis labels): 21.19s vs 3.3s P95 (record-from-docs) · ~5s vs ~500ms static (record-from-docs) · 48/53 = 90.5% migrated (record-from-docs; the source prints 90.5%, exact 90.57% — keep the lower, printed figure) · 11 partners / 10 countries MVP (record — matches S12 canon) · fix sequence WAF → double-CDN → regional CDN → China → Asia with smoke tests (record-from-docs). Never: a post-fix latency number (none in the record); never present scheduled items as delivered.
